SPECIAL  INTRODUCTION

THE  great  bibliographer,  Antonio,  cites  a  witty  remark
of  a  Spanish  orator  to  the  effect  that  the  index  of  a
book  should  be  made  by  the  author,  even  if  the  book
itself  were  written  by  someone  else.  Presumably,  the  Span-
iard in  question  had  suffered  from  ludicrous  errors  made  by
an  incompetent  literary  hack  employed  to  index  the  reports
of  his  speeches.  Nevertheless,  Lord  Campbell  seemed  to  be
of  the  opinion  that  any  kind  of  an  index  is  better  than  no
index  at  all,  as  he  proposed  that  any  author  who  published  a
book  without  an  index  should  not  only  be  deprived  of  the  bene-
fit of  a  Copyright  Act,  but  should  also  be  subjected  for  his
offence  to  a  pecuniary  penalty.

The  Index  Society  of  England  has  defined  the  meaning  of
the  word  "  index  "  to  be  "  an  indicator  or  pointer-out  of  the
position  or  location  of  required  in  formation,"  and  the  very
first  rule  of  the  Society  insists  that  everv  work  should  have
one  index  to  the  whole  set,  and  not  an  index  to  each  volume.
This  method,  which  has  been  adopted  for  the  Index  of  "  The
World's  Great  Classics/'  offers  the  advantage  of  having  the
topics,  names  of  persons,  places,  etc.,  treated  or  mentioned  in
any  volume  of  the  entire  series,  united  in  a  single  alphabet
and  in  classified  lists.  A  full  list  of  the  works  comprised  in
this  series  is  presented  elsewhere  in  the  form  of  a  tabular
summary.

A  library  of  sixty  volumes  would  be  almost  useless  for  busy
people  without  the  time-saving  medium  of  a  lucid,  practical,
and  comprehensive  index.  It  is  with  this  object  in  view  that
the  present  Index  has  been  constructed.  A  glance  through
the  four  indexes  * — The  General  Index,  the  Subject  Index,
the   Index  of   Authors,   and   the   Chronological   Index — will

♦English  grammars  usually  draw  the  distinction  that  indexes  is  the  form  used
in  speaking  of  the  plural  of  index,  as  a  book,  while  indices  is  a  scientific  term,
as  in  algebra.

•  •  •

in

iv  SPECIAL   INTRODUCTION

substantiate  the  correctness  of  this  statement.  The  reader  in
search  of  general  culture  will  find  all  the  essential  facts  and
data  of  History,  Philosophy,  Religion,  Law,  Political  Econ-
omy, Government,  Oratory,  the  Drama,  and  of  the  various
branches  of  Universal  Literature  represented  in  the  series,
fully  and  lucidly  indexed.

Opening  the  General  Index  at  random,  the  searcher  may
find  under  the  entry  of  Louis  XTV  that  this  monarch  is  men-
tioned, or  the  episodes  of  his  career  are  treated  at  consider-
able length,  in  British  Orations,  in  Charles  XII,  in  Civilization
in  Europe,  in  Classic  Memoirs,  Vol.  I,  in  Decisive  Battles  of
the  World,  in  Democracy  in  America,  in  The  French  Revolu-
tion, in  A  Short  History  of  the  English  People,  in  Modern
History,  and  in  The  Spirit  of  Laws.  The  reader  will  also
find  that  all  references  to  the  other  French  kings  of  the  name
of  Louis  are  indexed  in  a  similar  manner.  Thus,  in  con-
densed form  and  without  missing  an  important  detail,  the  po-
litical history  of  eighteen  kings  of  France  may  be  traced
within  the  space  of  three  pages.

Turning  to  the  Subject  Index  we  find,  under  Drama,  sub-
headings and  references  to  the  American  Drama,  the  Ar-
menian Drama,  the  Chinese  Drama,  the  English  Drama,
the  French  Drama,  the  German  Drama,  the  Greek  Drama,
the  Hindu  Drama,  the  Norwegian  Drama,  the  Japanese
Drama,  the  Spanish  Drama,  and  the  Turkish  Drama.
These  entries  all  accord  with  the  plan  and  scope  of  the  Sub-
ject Index,  in  which  the  various  topics  discussed  and  treated
in  the  series  are  presented  under  classified  headings.  Here,
under  Germany,  will  be  found  the  sub-headings  of  History
and  Literature,  and  under  each  of  these  sub-headings  are
citations  of  the  various  events  or  subjects  mentioned  or  dis-
cussed that  relate  to  the  history  or  literature  of  that  country.
So,  again,  under  the  head  of  Government  and  Law  are  all  ref-
erences to  that  extensive  subject  embodied  in  any  volume  of
the  series.  The  Subject  Index,  moreover,  also  serves  as  an
alphabetical  list  of  contents.

An  examination  of  the  Index  of  Authors  will  show  the  full
names  of  all  authors,  presented  in  alphabetical  order,  together
with  the  works,  the  Special  Introductions,  or  the  Translations,
by  which  they  are  represented  in  the  series.

SPECIAL   INTRODUCTION  v

The  Chronological  Index,  arranged  in  the  sequence  of  years,
gives  the  dates  and  names  of  the  most  important  events  in
History,  Literature,  Law  and  Political  Economy,  Oratory,  and
Philosophy  and  Religion,  so  far  as  they  come  within  the  prov-
ince of  the  "Classics/'  and  covers  a  period  from  2000  b.c.
down  to  the  present  day.  Accordingly,  the  Chronological  In-
dex supplies  an  excellent  means  of  ascertaining  without  any
special  research  the  dates  of  noteworthy  events.  In  fact,  it
presents  in  tabular  form  a  bird's-eye  view,  as  it  were,  of  the
Political,  Literary,  and  Philosophic  History  of  the  World,  ob-
viating the  necessity  of  looking  -through  the  entire  sixty  vol-
umes to  obtain  the  desired  information.

The  Editors,  however,  have  kept  in  mind  that  the  mission
of  an  index  is  not  to  supply  the  place  of  the  book.  They  have
also  kept  in  mind  the  danger  of  profuse  cross-reference.  Mr.
W.  F.  Poole,  the  expert  indexer  of  periodical  literature,  has
well  said  that  if  every  title-word  should  have  cross-references
to  its  allies  the  work  would  be  mainly  a  book  of  cross-refer-
ences and  not  an  index  to  a  book.  The  Editors,  .therefore,
have  endeavored  to  eliminate  everything  not  of  actual  value
in  the  matter  of  reference,  and  they  have  tried  to  include  every
title-word  that  would  be  likely  to  occur  to  a  reader  in  search
of  unlocated  information.

The  labor  of  such  a  task,  in  covering  more  than  sixty  dif-
ferent volumes,  may  be  readily  conceived,  and  in  sending  forth
this  Index  the  Editors  trust  that  the  work  of  many  months,
and  of  much  careful  planning,  will  make  the  masterpieces  of
literature  contained  in  "  The  World's  Great  Classics  "  more
accessible,  more  valuable,  and  more  attractive  to  the  reader.
The  soul  of  a  book  should  be  found  in  the  index.  As  the  hilt
is  to  the  sword,  so  is  the  index  to  the  book.

In  conclusion,  the  Editors  desire  to  express  their  thanks  to
Mr.  Elford  E.  Treffry  and  the  entire  corps  of  professional
indexers  who  have  devoted  months  of  patient  labor  to  the
compilation  of  the  present  volume,  and  also  to  Mr.  Robert
Arnot,  Mr.  Chauncey  C.  Starkweather,  and  Mr.  John  H.
Clifford,  for  their  work  of  editorial  revision.  A  word  of  ac-
knowledgment is  likewise  due  to  the  corps  of  young  women
from  the  Astor  Librarv  who  carried  out  the  work  of  transcrib-
ing  titles  on  cards,  and  the  intricate  work  of  alphabetization,

vi  SPECIAL   INTRODUCTION

with  commendable  skill.  While  it  is  not  claimed  that  the
present  volume  contains  the  most  exhaustive  index  ever  made,
it  may  be  claimed,  without  fear  of  contradiction,  that  there  is
no  index  in  existence  that  in  itself  covers  so  many  classics.
In  the  language  of  old-fashioned  prefaces,  it  may  be  said  that
it  supplies  "  a  long-felt  want."  At  all  events,  the  reader  will
search  in  vain  for  any  index  of  greater  usefulness  for  pur-
poses of  reference  in  the  field  that  it  claims  to  cover.

beginning  on  page  ix.

When  a  work  has  more  than  one  volume  the  number  of  the  volume  is  indicated  in  the  Index
by  a  Roman  numeral.  Thus  "  Classic  Memoirs "  vol.  I.  appears  in  the  Index  as  Classic
Memoirs,  i ;  "  Classic  Memoirs"  vol.  II.  as  Classic  Memoirs,  it.,  and  "Classic  Memoirs"  vol.
III.  as  Classic  Memoirs,  iiu

In  the  Genera]  Index,  whenever  the  same  title-word  calls  for  credit  from  several  volumes
respectively,  as,  for  instance,  on  page  368  under  the  title-word  War,  the  titles  of  volumes  have
been  alphabetized  as  in  the  above  list.  The  only  departure  from  this  rule  in  the  General  Index
is  where  the  same  title  word  refers  both  to  a  place  and  to  a  person,  as  in  the  case  of  the  title-
word  Orleans  on  page  265.  In  such  cases  the  references  to  the  place  are  alphabetized  together
and  precede  the  references  to  the  person  or  persons,  the  crediting  volumes  being  alphabetized,
of  course,  in  corresponding  manner.
